People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I end up worn out and still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I stopped taking paid carpentry jobs because I can’t get out of the house and get through a day normally. I used to help with community projects too, but I’ve pulled back from that.\n\nMy wife has had to take on more errands and decisions because I get stuck checking things at home, and by the time I’m done I’m just exhausted.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting stuck checking things at home. Outlets, locks, tools, even dust… I go over them again and again, trying to fix or make sure everything is right. It takes up most of my day, and even when I’m worn out, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the need to check things?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I know I did something right, I get these awful thoughts that maybe I missed a loose wire and the house could burn down, or that I left a nail somewhere and my wife might get hurt. I try to push the thoughts away, but they just come back harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Yes, kind of. I don’t know if it’s always a set order, but I get stuck going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I keep checking or fixing them again and again. I can spend most of the day doing that until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these checks?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what it feels like. I keep going back over the outlets, locks, tools, and even dust because I’m trying to make sure nothing bad happens. I check and fix things again and again, most of the day sometimes, and even when I’m exhausted I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed one of these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a minute, but not really in a way that lasts. I’ll check the outlets or locks or the tools, and I think I should be done, but then I still don’t feel sure. So I go back and do it again, and again, until I’m worn out. It takes up most of my day sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to check things a certain number of times to feel okay about stopping?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I don’t feel sure enough to stop. I’ll check or fix something, then doubt it, and do it again until I’m worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to arrange things in a specific way, like keeping your tools or other items in a particular order?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my tools and things around the house. It’s not like they have to be in some perfect order, but I keep checking them and fixing them, moving them back, making sure they’re right. Same with outlets, locks, even dust. I go over it again and again, and it takes up most of my day sometimes. I end up worn out and still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I stopped taking paid carpentry jobs because I can’t get out of the house and get through a day normally. I used to help with community projects too, but I’ve pulled back from that.\n\nMy wife has had to take on more errands and decisions because I get stuck checking things at home, and by the time I’m done I’m just exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the tools or items you check need to be in a perfect or symmetrical order?\n\nPatient: Not really perfect or symmetrical, no. It’s more that I keep going back to the same things—my tools, outlets, locks, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again. I just don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone, and I end up worn out from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to repeat a checking action a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, my tools, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again until I’m worn out. I just still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting stuck checking things at home. Outlets, locks, tools, even dust… I go over them again and again, trying to fix or make sure everything is right. It takes up most of my day, and even when I’m worn out, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the need to check things?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I know I did something right, I get these awful thoughts that maybe I missed a loose wire and the house could burn down, or that I left a nail somewhere and my wife might get hurt. I try to push the thoughts away, but they just come back harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Yes, kind of. I don’t know if it’s always a set order, but I get stuck going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I keep checking or fixing them again and again. I can spend most of the day doing that until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these checks?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what it feels like. I keep going back over the outlets, locks, tools, and even dust because I’m trying to make sure nothing bad happens. I check and fix things again and again, most of the day sometimes, and even when I’m exhausted I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed one of these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a minute, but not really in a way that lasts. It takes up most of my day sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to check things a certain number of times to feel okay about stopping?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I don’t feel sure enough to stop. I’ll check or fix something, then doubt it, and do it again until I’m worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to arrange things in a specific way, like keeping your tools or other items in a particular order?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my tools and things around the house. It’s not like they have to be in some perfect order, but I keep checking them and fixing them, moving them back, making sure they’re right. Same with outlets, locks, even dust. I go over it again and again, and it takes up most of my day sometimes. I end up worn out and still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I stopped taking paid carpentry jobs because I can’t get out of the house and get through a day normally. I used to help with community projects too, but I’ve pulled back from that.\n\nMy wife has had to take on more errands and decisions because I get stuck checking things at home, and by the time I’m done I’m just exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the tools or items you check need to be in a perfect or symmetrical order?\n\nPatient: Not really perfect or symmetrical, no. It’s more that I keep going back to the same things—my tools, outlets, locks, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again. I just don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone, and I end up worn out from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to repeat a checking action a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, my tools, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again until I’m worn out. I just still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or arrange need to be 'just right' in some way?\n\nPatient: Not exactly “just right” like neat or perfect. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting stuck checking things at home. Outlets, locks, tools, even dust… I go over them again and again, trying to fix or make sure everything is right. It takes up most of my day, and even when I’m worn out, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the need to check things?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I know I did something right, I get these awful thoughts that maybe I missed a loose wire and the house could burn down, or that I left a nail somewhere and my wife might get hurt. I try to push the thoughts away, but they just come back harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Yes, kind of. I don’t know if it’s always a set order, but I get stuck going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I keep checking or fixing them again and again. I can spend most of the day doing that until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these checks?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what it feels like. I keep going back over the outlets, locks, tools, and even dust because I’m trying to make sure nothing bad happens. I check and fix things again and again, most of the day sometimes, and even when I’m exhausted I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed one of these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a minute, but not really in a way that lasts. I’ll check the outlets or locks or the tools, and I think I should be done, but then I still don’t feel sure. So I go back and do it again, and again, until I’m worn out. It takes up most of my day sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to check things a certain number of times to feel okay about stopping?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I don’t feel sure enough to stop. I’ll check or fix something, then doubt it, and do it again until I’m worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to arrange things in a specific way, like keeping your tools or other items in a particular order?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my tools and things around the house. It’s not like they have to be in some perfect order, but I keep checking them and fixing them, moving them back, making sure they’re right. Same with outlets, locks, even dust. I go over it again and again, and it takes up most of my day sometimes. I end up worn out and still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I stopped taking paid carpentry jobs because I can’t get out of the house and get through a day normally. I used to help with community projects too, but I’ve pulled back from that.\n\nMy wife has had to take on more errands and decisions because I get stuck checking things at home, and by the time I’m done I’m just exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the tools or items you check need to be in a perfect or symmetrical order?\n\nPatient: Not really perfect or symmetrical, no. It’s more that I keep going back to the same things—my tools, outlets, locks, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again. I just don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone, and I end up worn out from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to repeat a checking action a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, my tools, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again until I’m worn out. I just still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or arrange need to be 'just right' in some way?\n\nPatient: Not exactly “just right” like neat or perfect. It’s more like I can’t trust that it’s okay. I keep going back to the outlets, locks, tools, and dust, checking or fixing them again and again until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t really feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat these checking actions in exactly the same way each time?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the same way each time. It’s more that I keep getting pulled back to the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I check them or fix them over and over. I try to push the thoughts away, but they just come back harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Yes, kind of. I don’t know if it’s always a set order, but I get stuck going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I keep checking or fixing them again and again. I can spend most of the day doing that until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these checks?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what it feels like. I keep going back over the outlets, locks, tools, and even dust because I’m trying to make sure nothing bad happens. I check and fix things again and again, most of the day sometimes, and even when I’m exhausted I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed one of these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a minute, but not really in a way that lasts. I’ll check the outlets or locks or the tools, and I think I should be done, but then I still don’t feel sure. So I go back and do it again, and again, until I’m worn out. It takes up most of my day sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to check things a certain number of times to feel okay about stopping?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I don’t feel sure enough to stop. I’ll check or fix something, then doubt it, and do it again until I’m worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to arrange things in a specific way, like keeping your tools or other items in a particular order?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my tools and things around the house. It’s not like they have to be in some perfect order, but I keep checking them and fixing them, moving them back, making sure they’re right. Same with outlets, locks, even dust. I go over it again and again, and it takes up most of my day sometimes. I end up worn out and still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I stopped taking paid carpentry jobs because I can’t get out of the house and get through a day normally. I used to help with community projects too, but I’ve pulled back from that.\n\nMy wife has had to take on more errands and decisions because I get stuck checking things at home, and by the time I’m done I’m just exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the tools or items you check need to be in a perfect or symmetrical order?\n\nPatient: Not really perfect or symmetrical, no. It’s more that I keep going back to the same things—my tools, outlets, locks, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again. I just don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone, and I end up worn out from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to repeat a checking action a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, my tools, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again until I’m worn out. I just still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or arrange need to be 'just right' in some way?\n\nPatient: Not exactly “just right” like neat or perfect. It’s more like I can’t trust that it’s okay. I keep going back to the outlets, locks, tools, and dust, checking or fixing them again and again until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t really feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat these checking actions in exactly the same way each time?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the same way each time. It’s more that I keep getting pulled back to the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I check them or fix them over and over. I can spend most of the day doing that, and even when I’m worn out, I still don’t feel sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about germs or getting sick when you're checking these things at home?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s really about germs or getting sick. At least, that’s not what I notice. It’s more just… I don’t feel sure, so I check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a specific pattern or arrangement?\n\nPatient: Not really a specific pattern or arrangement. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—checking or fixing them again and again. I can spend most of the day doing it, and even when I’m worn out, I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting stuck checking things at home. Outlets, locks, tools, even dust… I go over them again and again, trying to fix or make sure everything is right. It takes up most of my day, and even when I’m worn out, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the need to check things?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I know I did something right, I get these awful thoughts that maybe I missed a loose wire and the house could burn down, or that I left a nail somewhere and my wife might get hurt. I try to push the thoughts away, but they just come back harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Yes, kind of. I don’t know if it’s always a set order, but I get stuck going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I keep checking or fixing them again and again. I can spend most of the day doing that until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these checks?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what it feels like. I keep going back over the outlets, locks, tools, and even dust because I’m trying to make sure nothing bad happens. I check and fix things again and again, most of the day sometimes, and even when I’m exhausted I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed one of these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a minute, but not really in a way that lasts. I’ll check the outlets or locks or the tools, and I think I should be done, but then I still don’t feel sure. So I go back and do it again, and again, until I’m worn out. It takes up most of my day sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to check things a certain number of times to feel okay about stopping?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I don’t feel sure enough to stop. I’ll check or fix something, then doubt it, and do it again until I’m worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to arrange things in a specific way, like keeping your tools or other items in a particular order?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my tools and things around the house. It’s not like they have to be in some perfect order, but I keep checking them and fixing them, moving them back, making sure they’re right. Same with outlets, locks, even dust. I go over it again and again, and it takes up most of my day sometimes. I end up worn out and still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I stopped taking paid carpentry jobs because I can’t get out of the house and get through a day normally. I used to help with community projects too, but I’ve pulled back from that.\n\nMy wife has had to take on more errands and decisions because I get stuck checking things at home, and by the time I’m done I’m just exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the tools or items you check need to be in a perfect or symmetrical order?\n\nPatient: Not really perfect or symmetrical, no. It’s more that I keep going back to the same things—my tools, outlets, locks, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again. I just don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone, and I end up worn out from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to repeat a checking action a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, my tools, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again until I’m worn out. I just still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or arrange need to be 'just right' in some way?\n\nPatient: Not exactly “just right” like neat or perfect. It’s more like I can’t trust that it’s okay. I keep going back to the outlets, locks, tools, and dust, checking or fixing them again and again until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t really feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat these checking actions in exactly the same way each time?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the same way each time. It’s more that I keep getting pulled back to the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I check them or fix them over and over. I can spend most of the day doing that, and even when I’m worn out, I still don’t feel sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about germs or getting sick when you're checking these things at home?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s really about germs or getting sick. At least, that’s not what I notice. It’s more just… I don’t feel sure, so I check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a specific pattern or arrangement?\n\nPatient: Not really a specific pattern or arrangement. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—checking or fixing them again and again. I can spend most of the day doing it, and even when I’m worn out, I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts about harming yourself or someone else?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to hurt myself or anybody else. But I do get these ugly thoughts, like… even when I know I fixed something right, I suddenly think maybe I missed a loose wire and the house will burn down, or I left a nail somewhere and my wife could get hurt. I try to push it out of my head, but it comes back stronger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be symmetrical or look a certain way for you to feel okay about stopping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Not really arranged, no. It’s not like I need things lined up a certain way. I just keep going back to the same outlets, locks, tools, and dust, checking or fixing them over and over. It takes up most of my day, and even when I’m exhausted, I still don’t feel sure enough to stop.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting stuck checking things at home. Outlets, locks, tools, even dust… I go over them again and again, trying to fix or make sure everything is right. Even when I know I did something right, I get these awful thoughts that maybe I missed a loose wire and the house could burn down, or that I left a nail somewhere and my wife might get hurt. I try to push the thoughts away, but they just come back harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Yes, kind of. I don’t know if it’s always a set order, but I get stuck going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I keep checking or fixing them again and again. I can spend most of the day doing that until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these checks?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what it feels like. I keep going back over the outlets, locks, tools, and even dust because I’m trying to make sure nothing bad happens. I check and fix things again and again, most of the day sometimes, and even when I’m exhausted I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed one of these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a minute, but not really in a way that lasts. I’ll check the outlets or locks or the tools, and I think I should be done, but then I still don’t feel sure. So I go back and do it again, and again, until I’m worn out. It takes up most of my day sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to check things a certain number of times to feel okay about stopping?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I don’t feel sure enough to stop. I’ll check or fix something, then doubt it, and do it again until I’m worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to arrange things in a specific way, like keeping your tools or other items in a particular order?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my tools and things around the house. It’s not like they have to be in some perfect order, but I keep checking them and fixing them, moving them back, making sure they’re right. Same with outlets, locks, even dust. I go over it again and again, and it takes up most of my day sometimes. I end up worn out and still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I stopped taking paid carpentry jobs because I can’t get out of the house and get through a day normally. I used to help with community projects too, but I’ve pulled back from that.\n\nMy wife has had to take on more errands and decisions because I get stuck checking things at home, and by the time I’m done I’m just exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the tools or items you check need to be in a perfect or symmetrical order?\n\nPatient: Not really perfect or symmetrical, no. It’s more that I keep going back to the same things—my tools, outlets, locks, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again. I just don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone, and I end up worn out from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to repeat a checking action a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, my tools, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again until I’m worn out. At least, that’s not what I notice. It’s more just… I don’t feel sure, so I check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a specific pattern or arrangement?\n\nPatient: Not really a specific pattern or arrangement. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—checking or fixing them again and again. I can spend most of the day doing it, and even when I’m worn out, I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts about harming yourself or someone else?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to hurt myself or anybody else. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I don’t feel sure enough to stop. I’ll check or fix something, then doubt it, and do it again until I’m worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to arrange things in a specific way, like keeping your tools or other items in a particular order?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my tools and things around the house. It’s not like they have to be in some perfect order, but I keep checking them and fixing them, moving them back, making sure they’re right. Same with outlets, locks, even dust. I go over it again and again, and it takes up most of my day sometimes. I end up worn out and still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I stopped taking paid carpentry jobs because I can’t get out of the house and get through a day normally. I used to help with community projects too, but I’ve pulled back from that.\n\nMy wife has had to take on more errands and decisions because I get stuck checking things at home, and by the time I’m done I’m just exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the tools or items you check need to be in a perfect or symmetrical order?\n\nPatient: Not really perfect or symmetrical, no. It’s more that I keep going back to the same things—my tools, outlets, locks, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again. I just don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone, and I end up worn out from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to repeat a checking action a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, my tools, dust—and checking or fixing them again and again until I’m worn out. I just still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or arrange need to be 'just right' in some way?\n\nPatient: Not exactly “just right” like neat or perfect. It’s more like I can’t trust that it’s okay. I keep going back to the outlets, locks, tools, and dust, checking or fixing them again and again until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t really feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat these checking actions in exactly the same way each time?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the same way each time. It’s more that I keep getting pulled back to the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—and I check them or fix them over and over. I can spend most of the day doing that, and even when I’m worn out, I still don’t feel sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about germs or getting sick when you're checking these things at home?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s really about germs or getting sick. At least, that’s not what I notice. It’s more just… I don’t feel sure, so I check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a specific pattern or arrangement?\n\nPatient: Not really a specific pattern or arrangement. It’s more that I keep going back over the same things—outlets, locks, tools, dust—checking or fixing them again and again. I can spend most of the day doing it, and even when I’m worn out, I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts about harming yourself or someone else?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to hurt myself or anybody else. But I do get these ugly thoughts, like… even when I know I fixed something right, I suddenly think maybe I missed a loose wire and the house will burn down, or I left a nail somewhere and my wife could get hurt. I try to push it out of my head, but it comes back stronger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be symmetrical or look a certain way for you to feel okay about stopping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed it being about symmetry or how it looks, exactly. It’s more just whether I can feel okay enough to stop, and I usually can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be arranged in a way that looks or feels complete to you?\n\nPatient: Not really arranged, no. It’s not like I need things lined up a certain way. I just keep going back to the same outlets, locks, tools, and dust, checking or fixing them over and over. It takes up most of my day, and even when I’m exhausted, I still don’t feel sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat these checking actions until you feel like you've done them a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a number, no.
